    \section{6. Exploratory Data Analysis}\label{exploratory-data-analysis}

Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA) is the systematic process of examining a
dataset to understand its structure, distributions, relationships,
patterns, and unusual observations.

In this phase, we will investigate the selected positive sales candidate
population.

Our analysis will combine:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Quantitative EDA: descriptive statistics, frequency distributions,
  proportions, quantiles, and association measures.
\item
  Visual EDA: distribution plots, categorical comparisons, relationship
  plots, and multivariate visualizations.
\item
  Business interpretation: translating statistical observations into
  meaningful insights about the retailer's merchandise activity.
\end{itemize}

We will distinguish between observations, interpretations, and
conclusions. Patterns in observational data do not automatically
establish causation.

    \subsection{6.1 Understanding the Analytical
Variables}\label{understanding-the-analytical-variables}

Before calculating statistics or creating visualizations, we need to
understand the variables available in our analytical dataset.

Different variables require different analytical techniques.

For example:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Numerical variables can be investigated using descriptive statistics
  and distribution analysis.
\item
  Categorical variables require frequency distributions and category
  comparisons.
\item
  Temporal variables allow us to investigate changes across dates,
  months, weekdays, and hours.
\item
  Identifiers help us distinguish invoices, products, and customers but
  should not ordinarily be interpreted as numerical measurements.
\item
  Derived variables provide additional analytical dimensions while
  retaining their relationship with the original observations.
\end{itemize}

We will first classify the available variables according to their
analytical roles.

    \subsubsection{6.1.1 Classifying Variables by Analytical
Role}\label{classifying-variables-by-analytical-role}

We will classify the variables into the following groups:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Identifiers:} Variables identifying invoices, products, or
  customers.
\item
  \textbf{Categorical:} Variables representing groups or labels.
\item
  \textbf{Numerical:} Variables representing quantities or monetary
  values.
\item
  \textbf{Temporal:} Variables representing dates and components of
  time.
\item
  \textbf{Boolean/diagnostic:} Variables representing conditions or
  classification flags.
\end{enumerate}

A variable's pandas dtype does not necessarily determine its analytical
role. For example, CustomerID may be stored as a string even though it
identifies individual customers.

    \subsubsection{6.1.2 Establishing the Analytical
Questions}\label{establishing-the-analytical-questions}

The variable inventory helps us establish the main questions that will
guide our EDA.

\textbf{Transaction and quantity analysis} - How are transaction
quantities distributed? - Are quantities concentrated in a small number
of products or transactions? - What are the typical and extreme
quantities?

\textbf{Pricing and monetary analysis} - How are unit prices
distributed? - What is the distribution of invoice-line values? - Are
monetary values dominated by a small number of observations?

\textbf{Product analysis} - Which products appear most frequently? -
Which products contribute most to quantities and line values? - How
concentrated is merchandise activity across products?

\textbf{Customer analysis} - How many transactions and products are
associated with identifiable customers? - How is activity distributed
across customers? - Are a small number of customers responsible for a
substantial share of activity?

\textbf{Geographic analysis} - How is merchandise activity distributed
across countries? - How concentrated are quantities and line values
geographically?

\textbf{Temporal analysis} - How does activity vary over time? - Are
there differences across months, weekdays, and hours?

These questions provide direction rather than predetermined conclusions.
We will let the data guide the investigation.

    \subsubsection{6.1.3 Identifying Constant and Variable
Features}\label{identifying-constant-and-variable-features}

A variable is constant within a dataset when it contains only one
distinct non-missing value.

Constant variables provide no information about variation within the
selected population. Their presence is not necessarily a data-quality
problem: some variables become constant as a direct consequence of
analytical population selection.

We will identify constant and non-constant variables.

We will retain all original columns in the dataset but exclude constant
variables from analyses where they provide no useful information.

    \subsubsection{6.1.4 Interpreting Feature
Variability}\label{interpreting-feature-variability}

Constant variables are retained for documentation and reproducibility
but will not be treated as meaningful analytical dimensions within this
population.

Non-constant variables will form the basis of our subsequent EDA.

This distinction prevents redundant statistics and uninformative
visualizations while preserving the complete analytical dataset.

    \subsubsection{6.1.5 Organizing Variables into Analytical
Groups}\label{organizing-variables-into-analytical-groups}

We will organize the non-constant variables into five analytical groups.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Numerical variables}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Discrete: Quantity
  \item
    Continuous: UnitPrice, LineValue
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Categorical variables}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Country
  \item
    Description
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Identifiers}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    InvoiceNo
  \item
    StockCode
  \item
    CustomerID
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Temporal variables}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    InvoiceDate
  \item
    InvoiceYear
  \item
    InvoiceMonth
  \item
    InvoiceDay
  \item
    DayOfWeekNumber
  \item
    DayOfWeek
  \item
    InvoiceHour
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Boolean variables}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    IsWeekend
  \item
    HasCustomerID
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

These groups are based on analytical roles rather than pandas storage
types.

For example, StockCode is an identifier, while Country is a nominal
categorical variable. Both may be stored as strings, but they require
different analytical treatments.

\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}

\PY{n}{grouped\PYZus{}variables} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}
    \PY{n}{variable}

    \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{variable} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{variables}
\PY{p}{]}

\PY{k}{assert} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{grouped\PYZus{}variables}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{==} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{set}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{grouped\PYZus{}variables}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{k}{assert} \PY{n+nb}{set}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{grouped\PYZus{}variables}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{==} \PY{n+nb}{set}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{variable\PYZus{}columns}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{variable\PYZus{}group\PYZus{}inventory} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}
    \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{analytical\PYZus{}group}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{group}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{variable}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{variable}\PY{p}{,}

        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{unique\PYZus{}values}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{variable}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{nunique}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}

\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{6.1.6 Preparing for Univariate
Analysis}\label{preparing-for-univariate-analysis}

With the variable groups established, we can plan the appropriate
techniques for each group.

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Variable group

Numerical & Descriptive statistics, quantiles, skewness, kurtosis &
Histograms, box plots, KDE plots \\
Categorical & Frequency, proportion, cardinality & Bar charts, Pareto
charts \\
Identifiers & Distinct counts, frequency, concentration & Ranked
frequency charts \\
Temporal & Frequency across time components, time-based aggregation &
Line charts, seasonal comparisons \\
Boolean & Counts and proportions & Bar charts \\
\end{longtable}
}

Not every variable requires every technique.

For example, plotting a histogram of InvoiceNo would not be meaningful
because invoice identifiers are not numerical measurements.

Similarly, high-cardinality variables such as Description may require
ranked frequency analysis rather than displaying every category
simultaneously.

We will choose methods according to the statistical properties and
business meaning of each variable.

    \subsection{6.2 Univariate Quantitative
Analysis}\label{univariate-quantitative-analysis}

Univariate quantitative analysis examines one numerical variable at a
time.

We will investigate the numerical characteristics of Quantity,
UnitPrice, and LineValue.

Our analysis will cover:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Central tendency: mean, median, and mode.
\item
  Dispersion: variance, standard deviation, range, and interquartile
  range.
\item
  Distribution: quantiles, skewness, and kurtosis.
\item
  Extreme observations: minimum, maximum, and potential outliers.
\end{itemize}

    \subsubsection{6.2.1 Descriptive
Statistics}\label{descriptive-statistics}

We begin with standard descriptive statistics.

For each numerical variable, we will calculate:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Count
\item
  Mean
\item
  Standard deviation
\item
  Minimum and maximum
\item
  1st, 5th, 25th, 50th, 75th, 95th, and 99th percentiles
\end{itemize}

The 50th percentile is the median. The 25th and 75th percentiles define
the interquartile range:

IQR = Q3 - Q1

The IQR describes the spread of the middle 50\% of observations and is
less sensitive to extreme values than the standard deviation.

\prompt{In}{incolor}{58}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{QUANTITATIVE\PYZus{}VARIABLES} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Quantity}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{UnitPrice}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{LineValue}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{]}

\PY{n}{quantitative\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}
    \PY{n}{QUANTITATIVE\PYZus{}VARIABLES}
\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{describe}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{percentiles}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{[}
        \PY{l+m+mf}{0.01}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+m+mf}{0.25}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+m+mf}{0.50}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+m+mf}{0.75}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+m+mf}{0.95}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+m+mf}{0.99}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{]}
\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{T}

\PY{n}{quantitative\PYZus{}summary}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{variance}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{QUANTITATIVE\PYZus{}VARIABLES}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{var}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{quantitative\PYZus{}summary}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{IQR}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}

\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{6.2.2 Interpreting Descriptive
Statistics}\label{interpreting-descriptive-statistics}

We will examine the results variable by variable.

Particular attention should be paid to differences between means and
medians.

A substantial difference may indicate an asymmetric distribution.

We will also compare standard deviations and IQRs to understand how
sensitive each variable's dispersion is to extreme observations.

Percentiles help us understand the distribution beyond its center and
identify whether unusually large values are concentrated in the upper
tail.

We will avoid interpreting the results as representative of all retailer
operations because our analysis uses the selected positive sales
candidate population.

    \subsubsection{6.2.3 Skewness and Kurtosis}\label{skewness-and-kurtosis}

We will calculate two additional distribution measures.

\textbf{Skewness} measures distributional asymmetry.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Positive skewness: a longer or heavier right tail.
\item
  Negative skewness: a longer or heavier left tail.
\item
  Skewness near zero: approximately symmetric distribution, although
  this does not guarantee symmetry.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Kurtosis} describes tail heaviness relative to a normal
distribution when using the conventional Pearson definition.

Pandas' \texttt{Series.kurt()} calculates excess kurtosis:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Normal distribution: excess kurtosis of approximately zero.
\item
  Positive excess kurtosis: heavier tails or greater concentration
  around the center relative to a normal distribution.
\item
  Negative excess kurtosis: lighter tails relative to a normal
  distribution.
\end{itemize}

These statistics are sensitive to extreme observations. We will
interpret them alongside distribution plots rather than in isolation.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{59}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{distribution\PYZus{}shape\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{skewness}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{QUANTITATIVE\PYZus{}VARIABLES}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{skew}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{excess\PYZus{}kurtosis}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{QUANTITATIVE\PYZus{}VARIABLES}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{kurt}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{distribution\PYZus{}shape\PYZus{}summary}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \subsubsection{6.2.4 Visualizing Numerical
Distributions}\label{visualizing-numerical-distributions}

Because all three variables are strictly positive and strongly
right-skewed, we will use base-10 logarithmic transformations for the
histograms.

For a positive variable X, the transformation is:

log10(X)

This compresses large values and makes the distribution's lower and
middle ranges easier to inspect.

The transformed values are used only for visualization. We will retain
the original measurements for all subsequent calculations and business
interpretations.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\PY{n}{axes} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{fig}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{subplots}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{ax}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{column} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n+nb}{zip}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{axes}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{QUANTITATIVE\PYZus{}VARIABLES}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}

    \PY{n}{sns}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{histplot}\PY{p}{(}

        \PY{n}{kde}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{ax}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{set\PYZus{}title}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Distribution of }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{column}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{grid}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{axis}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{y}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{alpha}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.3}\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{6.2.5 Visualizing Dispersion with Box
Plots}\label{visualizing-dispersion-with-box-plots}

Histograms help us understand distributional shape. Box plots provide a
complementary view of central tendency, dispersion, and potential
extreme observations.

A standard box plot displays:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Median: the line inside the box.
\item
  First and third quartiles: the lower and upper boundaries of the box.
\item
  Interquartile range: the height of the box.
\item
  Whiskers: the range of observations within the conventional 1.5 × IQR
  criterion.
\item
  Fliers: observations beyond the whiskers.
\end{itemize}

Because our variables have extreme upper tails, we will use logarithmic
vertical axes.

We will hide individual fliers to keep the plots readable. This does not
remove observations from the dataset or change the box-and-whisker
calculations.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{61}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{fig} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{Figure}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{figsize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{15}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{axes} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{fig}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{subplots}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{ax}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{column} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n+nb}{zip}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{axes}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{QUANTITATIVE\PYZus{}VARIABLES}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{sns}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{boxplot}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{y}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{showfliers}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{ax}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{set\PYZus{}yscale}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{log}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{set\PYZus{}title}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Box Plot of }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{column}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{set\PYZus{}ylabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{column}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ (log scale)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{grid}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{axis}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{y}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{alpha}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.3}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{fig}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{tight\PYZus{}layout}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{6.2.6 Identifying Potential Outliers with the IQR
Rule}\label{identifying-potential-outliers-with-the-iqr-rule}

We will use the conventional 1.5 × IQR rule to identify potential
outliers in the three quantitative variables.

An observation is flagged when:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  It is below the lower fence, or
\item
  It is above the upper fence.
\end{itemize}

These are statistical flags, not proof of data errors.

This distinction is particularly important for our dataset because all
three variables have strongly right-skewed distributions. Legitimate
high-volume purchases, expensive products, and high-value line items may
be flagged.

We will quantify the flagged observations but will not remove them
automatically.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{62}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{column} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{QUANTITATIVE\PYZus{}VARIABLES}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{q1} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{quantile}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.25}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{q3} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{quantile}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.75}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{iqr} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{q3} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}} \PY{n}{q1}

    \PY{n}{lower\PYZus{}fence} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{q1} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}} \PY{l+m+mf}{1.5} \PY{o}{*} \PY{n}{iqr}
    \PY{n}{upper\PYZus{}fence} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{q3} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+m+mf}{1.5} \PY{o}{*} \PY{n}{iqr}

    \PY{n}{lower\PYZus{}outliers} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{n}{lower\PYZus{}fence}
    \PY{n}{upper\PYZus{}outliers} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{\PYZgt{}} \PY{n}{upper\PYZus{}fence}
    \PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}mask} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{lower\PYZus{}outliers} \PY{o}{|} \PY{n}{upper\PYZus{}outliers}

    \PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}count} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}mask}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}summary}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{append}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{variable}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Q1}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{q1}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Q3}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{q3}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{IQR}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{iqr}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{lower\PYZus{}fence}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{lower\PYZus{}fence}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{upper\PYZus{}fence}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{upper\PYZus{}fence}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{lower\PYZus{}outliers}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{lower\PYZus{}outliers}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{upper\PYZus{}outliers}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{upper\PYZus{}outliers}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{total\PYZus{}outliers}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}count}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{outlier\PYZus{}percentage}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{(}
            \PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}count} \PY{o}{/} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{*} \PY{l+m+mi}{100}
        \PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}summary}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{set\PYZus{}index}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{variable}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{outlier\PYZus{}summary}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \subsubsection{6.2.7 Investigating Extreme
Observations}\label{investigating-extreme-observations}

We will inspect the largest observations in each quantitative variable.

The purpose is not to remove extreme values, but to understand their
characteristics and determine whether they represent plausible business
activity, unusual transactions, or potential data-quality concerns.

We will retain the original dataset throughout this investigation.

    \subsubsection{6.2.8 Checking Extreme Quantities Against the Complete
Dataset}\label{checking-extreme-quantities-against-the-complete-dataset}

The two largest quantity observations are unusually large:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Invoice 581483, StockCode 23843: 80,995 units.
\item
  Invoice 541431, StockCode 23166: 74,215 units.
\end{itemize}

We will search the complete prepared dataset for these invoice numbers
and stock codes.

This investigation may help us determine whether these observations have
corresponding cancellation records or other unusual transaction
patterns.

We will not modify or remove any records.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

    \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{541431}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{23166}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{]}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}no}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{stock\PYZus{}code} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{extreme\PYZus{}cases}\PY{p}{:}

    \PY{n}{matching\PYZus{}records} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}analysis}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{loc}\PY{p}{[}

        \PY{p}{)}
    \PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{copy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{6.2.9 Investigating Cancellation Counterparts for Extreme
Quantities}\label{investigating-cancellation-counterparts-for-extreme-quantities}

We will identify positive sales candidates with unusually high
quantities and search the complete prepared dataset for matching
cancellation records.

A matching counterpart will require:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The same StockCode.
\item
  The same absolute Quantity.
\item
  The same UnitPrice.
\item
  The same absolute LineValue.
\item
  A cancellation invoice prefix (\texttt{C}).
\end{itemize}

This is a matching rule for investigation, not proof of a formal
relationship between invoice records.

    \subsubsection{6.2.10 Understanding the Structure of Cancellation
Records}\label{understanding-the-structure-of-cancellation-records}

Before attempting to link cancellations to original transactions, we
need to understand their characteristics.

We will examine:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Number of cancellation records and unique cancellation invoices.
\item
  Distribution of cancellation quantities and unit prices.
\item
  Number of cancellation records per invoice.
\item
  Whether cancellation invoices contain multiple product lines.
\end{enumerate}

This will help us determine whether invoice-level matching is feasible.

    \subsubsection{6.2.11 Examining Cancellation Invoice
Composition}\label{examining-cancellation-invoice-composition}

A cancellation invoice may contain one or multiple product lines.

If cancellation invoices contain multiple lines, matching should
consider the complete invoice composition rather than relying on an
isolated product and quantity.

We will calculate the number of records per cancellation invoice and
summarize its distribution.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{67}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{cancellation\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}sizes} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{cancellations}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{groupby}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{size}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{rename}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{line\PYZus{}count}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{cancellation\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}sizes}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{describe}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{percentiles}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.25}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.50}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.75}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.90}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.95}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.99}\PY{p}{]}

    \subsubsection{6.2.12 Testing Customer-Aware Cancellation
Matching}\label{testing-customer-aware-cancellation-matching}

We will attempt to associate cancellation records with earlier positive
sales candidates using:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  CustomerID
\item
  StockCode
\item
  Absolute Quantity
\item
  UnitPrice
\item
  Country
\item
  Transaction chronology
\end{itemize}

Only records with an identifiable CustomerID can participate in this
test.

For each cancellation line, we will count how many earlier positive
transaction lines satisfy these conditions.

Interpretation:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Zero candidates: no matching earlier transaction was found.
\item
  One candidate: one possible original transaction was found.
\item
  Multiple candidates: the association is ambiguous.
\end{itemize}

Even a unique candidate is not definitive proof of a cancellation
relationship.

    \subsubsection{6.2.13 Reviewing Candidate Matches and Time
Gaps}\label{reviewing-candidate-matches-and-time-gaps}

We will investigate two groups:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Cancellation lines with exactly one earlier candidate.
\item
  Cancellation lines with multiple earlier candidates.
\end{enumerate}

For unique candidates, we will calculate the elapsed time between the
two records.

For ambiguous cases, we will inspect several examples to understand why
multiple possible originals exist.

A shorter time gap may strengthen a possible association, but it is not
sufficient evidence to establish a definitive match.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{69}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{possible\PYZus{}matches}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{time\PYZus{}gap\PYZus{}hours}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{possible\PYZus{}matches}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{cancellation\PYZus{}date}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
    \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}} \PY{n}{possible\PYZus{}matches}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{original\PYZus{}date}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{dt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{total\PYZus{}seconds}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{/} \PY{l+m+mi}{3600}

\PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}ids} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{cancellation\PYZus{}match\PYZus{}results}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{loc}\PY{p}{[}
    \PY{n}{cancellation\PYZus{}match\PYZus{}results}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{candidate\PYZus{}count}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{cancellation\PYZus{}row\PYZus{}id}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{]}

\PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}matches} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{possible\PYZus{}matches}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{loc}\PY{p}{[}
    \PY{n}{possible\PYZus{}matches}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{cancellation\PYZus{}row\PYZus{}id}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{isin}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}ids}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{copy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Unique candidate time\PYZhy{}gap distribution:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}matches}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{time\PYZus{}gap\PYZus{}hours}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{describe}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{percentiles}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.25}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.50}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.75}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.90}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.95}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.99}\PY{p}{]}

    \subsubsection{6.2.15 Comparing Original and Cancellation Invoice
Composition}\label{comparing-original-and-cancellation-invoice-composition}

We will inspect selected original and cancellation invoices together.

The objective is to determine whether complete invoice composition
provides stronger evidence of cancellation relationships than individual
line matching.

We will compare: - Customer identity - Product codes - Quantities - Unit
prices - Line values - Invoice totals - Transaction timestamps

We will treat identical invoice compositions as stronger evidence, but
not as definitive proof of a formal linkage.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{71}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}pairs\PYZus{}to\PYZus{}inspect} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{538313}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{C538341}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{537140}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{C537143}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{581483}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{C581484}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{]}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{cancellation\PYZus{}invoice} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}pairs\PYZus{}to\PYZus{}inspect}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Original invoice: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Cancellation invoice: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{cancellation\PYZus{}invoice}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}records} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}analysis}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{loc}\PY{p}{[}
        \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}analysis}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{string}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{isin}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{cancellation\PYZus{}invoice}\PY{p}{]}
        \PY{p}{)}
    \PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{copy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}records}\PY{p}{[}
            \PY{p}{[}
                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CustomerID}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}

            \PY{p}{]}
        \PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sort\PYZus{}values}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{StockCode}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Quantity}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
        \PY{p}{)}
    \PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Invoice\PYZhy{}level summary:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}records}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{groupby}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{agg}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{n}{line\PYZus{}count}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{StockCode}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{size}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{n}{customer\PYZus{}count}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CustomerID}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{nunique}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{n}{stock\PYZus{}code\PYZus{}count}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{StockCode}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{nunique}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{n}{total\PYZus{}quantity}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Quantity}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{sum}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{n}{total\PYZus{}line\PYZus{}value}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{LineValue}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{sum}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}date}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceDate}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{min}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}

    \subsubsection{6.2.16 Identifying Full Invoice
Reversals}\label{identifying-full-invoice-reversals}

    \subsubsection{Building Aggregated Invoice
Compositions}\label{building-aggregated-invoice-compositions}

Repeated lines for the same stock code and unit price are combined
within each invoice.

For each invoice, we construct a composition signature containing: -
Stock code - Unit price - Aggregated quantity

For cancellation invoices, quantities are converted to absolute values
so their composition can be compared directly with positive invoices.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{72}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf}{build\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}compositions}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{cancellation}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{work} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{loc}\PY{p}{[}
        \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CustomerID}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{notna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{p}{[}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CustomerID}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Country}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceDate}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{StockCode}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Quantity}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{UnitPrice}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{copy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{work}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{work}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{string}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{work}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{UnitPriceRounded}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{work}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{UnitPrice}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{round}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{line\PYZus{}compositions} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{work}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{groupby}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{p}{[}
                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CustomerID}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Country}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{StockCode}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{UnitPriceRounded}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{n}{as\PYZus{}index}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{n}{observed}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{p}{)}
        \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{agg}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{aggregated\PYZus{}quantity}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Quantity}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{sum}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}dates} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{work}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{groupby}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceDate}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
        \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{min}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}records} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{]}

    \PY{k}{for} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{invoice}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{customer}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{country}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{group} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{line\PYZus{}compositions}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{groupby}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CustomerID}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Country}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{sort}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{observed}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{n}{signature} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n+nb}{tuple}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{n+nb}{sorted}\PY{p}{(}
                \PY{p}{(}
                    \PY{n+nb}{str}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{row}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{StockCode}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
                    \PY{n+nb}{float}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{row}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{UnitPriceRounded}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
                    \PY{n+nb}{abs}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{row}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{aggregated\PYZus{}quantity}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
                    \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{cancellation}
                    \PY{k}{else} \PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{row}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{aggregated\PYZus{}quantity}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
                \PY{p}{)}
                \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{row} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{group}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{itertuples}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
            \PY{p}{)}
        \PY{p}{)}

        \PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}records}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{append}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{invoice}\PY{p}{,}

                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{line\PYZus{}count\PYZus{}after\PYZus{}aggregation}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{group}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
                \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{composition\PYZus{}signature}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{signature}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
        \PY{p}{)}

    \PY{k}{return} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{invoice\PYZus{}records}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}compositions} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{build\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}compositions}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{Matching Full Invoice
Reversals}\label{matching-full-invoice-reversals}

We now compare each cancellation invoice against earlier positive-sales
invoices.

A candidate must have: 1. The same CustomerID. 2. The same Country. 3.
An identical aggregated product composition. 4. An earlier transaction
date.

We count distinct original invoice numbers rather than matching rows.

An exact composition match is strong evidence of a full reversal, but it
is still an inferred relationship rather than a documented invoice
reference.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{73}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{collections}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{defaultdict}

\PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}lookup} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{defaultdict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{list}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{row} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}compositions}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{itertuples}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{index}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{lookup\PYZus{}key} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{row}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{CustomerID}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{row}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{Country}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{row}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{composition\PYZus{}signature}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}lookup}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{lookup\PYZus{}key}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{append}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{row}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{InvoiceNo}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{row}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{InvoiceDate}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{full\PYZus{}reversal\PYZus{}candidates} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{cancellation} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{cancellation\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}compositions}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{itertuples}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{index}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{lookup\PYZus{}key} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{cancellation}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{CustomerID}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{cancellation}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{Country}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{cancellation}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{composition\PYZus{}signature}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{earlier\PYZus{}candidates} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}
        \PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice}
        \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{original\PYZus{}date}
        \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}lookup}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{get}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{lookup\PYZus{}key}\PY{p}{,} \PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
        \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{original\PYZus{}date} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{n}{cancellation}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{InvoiceDate}
    \PY{p}{]}

    \PY{n}{full\PYZus{}reversal\PYZus{}candidates}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{append}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{cancellation\PYZus{}invoice}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{cancellation}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{InvoiceNo}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{cancellation\PYZus{}date}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{cancellation}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{InvoiceDate}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{distinct\PYZus{}original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}count}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{earlier\PYZus{}candidates}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{candidate\PYZus{}original\PYZus{}invoices}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{earlier\PYZus{}candidates}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
    \PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{full\PYZus{}reversal\PYZus{}results} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{full\PYZus{}reversal\PYZus{}candidates}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{full\PYZus{}reversal\PYZus{}results}\PY{p}{[}
        \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{distinct\PYZus{}original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}count}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}
    \PY{p}{]}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{value\PYZus{}counts}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sort\PYZus{}index}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{rename\PYZus{}axis}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{candidate\PYZus{}count}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{to\PYZus{}frame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{cancellation\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}count}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{No candidate:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{full\PYZus{}reversal\PYZus{}results}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{distinct\PYZus{}original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}count}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Exactly one candidate:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{full\PYZus{}reversal\PYZus{}results}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{distinct\PYZus{}original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}count}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Multiple candidates:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{full\PYZus{}reversal\PYZus{}results}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{distinct\PYZus{}original\PYZus{}invoice\PYZus{}count}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{\PYZgt{}} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{Cancellation Matching Investigation ---
Conclusion}\label{cancellation-matching-investigation-conclusion}

An exploratory invoice-level matching analysis was conducted to identify
potential full invoice reversals.

Invoices were compared using customer identity, country, transaction
chronology, and aggregated product composition.

Among 3,654 cancellation invoices with identifiable customers: - 103 had
exactly one earlier full-composition candidate. - 7 had two candidates.
- 2 had three candidates. - 3,542 had no exact earlier full-composition
candidate.

These findings suggest that cancellation activity includes different
patterns, potentially including full reversals and partial
cancellations.

However, the dataset does not provide a definitive reference linking
cancellation invoices to their original invoices. Consequently, exact
transaction-level relationships cannot be established reliably.

The primary positive-sales-candidate population remains unchanged.
Cancellation records will be treated as a separate analytical population
rather than automatically deducted from positive transaction values.

All matching results are exploratory and should not be interpreted as
verified accounting or revenue adjustments.

    \subsection{6.3 Univariate Categorical
Analysis}\label{univariate-categorical-analysis}

This section examines the distribution of categorical variables and the
frequency of identified products.

We will focus on: - Geographic distribution by Country. - Product
distribution by StockCode.

The objective is to identify concentration, diversity, and dominant
categories within the positive-sales-candidate population.

    \subsubsection{Country Distribution}\label{country-distribution}

We will examine transaction records, invoices, identifiable customers,
quantities, and line values across countries.

Record percentages describe the distribution of invoice-line records,
not the percentage of customers or total sales.

    \subsubsection{Product Distribution}\label{product-distribution}

StockCode is used as the product identifier because product descriptions
are not guaranteed to be unique or consistent.

We will rank products by: - Number of invoice-line records. - Total
quantity. - Total line value.

These measures represent different aspects of product activity and
should not be treated as interchangeable.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{75}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{product\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{groupby}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{StockCode}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{observed}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{agg}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{Description}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Description}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{k}{lambda} \PY{n}{values}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{(}
                \PY{n}{values}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mode}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{iloc}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}
                \PY{k}{if} \PY{o+ow}{not} \PY{n}{values}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mode}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{empty}
                \PY{k}{else} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{NA}
            \PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{record\PYZus{}count}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{size}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}invoices}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{nunique}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}

        \PY{n}{total\PYZus{}quantity}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Quantity}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{sum}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}

    \PY{p}{)}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{product\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{product\PYZus{}summary}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sort\PYZus{}values}\PY{p}{(}

\PY{p}{)}

    \subsection{6.4 Temporal Exploratory Data
Analysis}\label{temporal-exploratory-data-analysis}

This section examines how positive-sales-candidate activity varies over
time.

We will analyze: - Monthly activity. - Day-of-week activity. - Hourly
activity.

We will compare transaction records, invoices, quantities, and line
values to understand different dimensions of temporal activity.

The dataset spans December 2010 to December 2011. December 2011 contains
only the first nine days, so it must be treated as a partial month.

    \subsubsection{Monthly Activity}\label{monthly-activity}

We aggregate the dataset by calendar month.

The analysis includes invoice-line records, distinct invoices,
identifiable customers, total quantity, and total line value.

Monthly comparisons describe observed activity in this dataset. They do
not independently establish business growth or seasonality.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{76}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{monthly\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{assign}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{YearMonth}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceDate}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{dt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{to\PYZus{}period}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{M}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{groupby}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{YearMonth}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{observed}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{agg}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{record\PYZus{}count}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{size}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}invoices}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{nunique}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}customers}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CustomerID}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{nunique}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{total\PYZus{}quantity}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Quantity}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{sum}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{total\PYZus{}line\PYZus{}value}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{LineValue}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{sum}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{p}{)}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{monthly\PYZus{}summary}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{round}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{Day-of-Week Activity}\label{day-of-week-activity}

We compare activity across weekdays.

The analysis can reveal whether transaction activity is distributed
evenly across the week or concentrated on particular days.

Only weekdays present in the dataset will appear in the summary.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{77}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Monday}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Tuesday}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Wednesday}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Thursday}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Friday}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Saturday}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sunday}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{]}

\PY{n}{weekday\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{groupby}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{DayOfWeek}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{observed}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{agg}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{record\PYZus{}count}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{size}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}invoices}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{nunique}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}customers}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CustomerID}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{nunique}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{total\PYZus{}quantity}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Quantity}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{sum}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}

\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{Hourly Activity}\label{hourly-activity}

We examine how invoice-line activity is distributed across the hours of
the day.

This helps identify periods of higher and lower recorded activity.

The analysis uses the transaction timestamps available in the dataset;
it does not establish store opening hours or staffing levels.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{78}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{hourly\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{groupby}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceHour}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{observed}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{agg}\PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{record\PYZus{}count}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{size}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}invoices}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{InvoiceNo}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{nunique}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{unique\PYZus{}customers}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CustomerID}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{nunique}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{n}{total\PYZus{}quantity}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Quantity}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{sum}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}

    \PY{p}{)}
    \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{reindex}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{range}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{24}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{fill\PYZus{}value}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{hourly\PYZus{}summary}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{round}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{Temporal Analysis --- Key
Findings}\label{temporal-analysis-key-findings}

Transaction activity varies considerably across months, weekdays, and
hours.

November 2011 recorded the highest monthly activity. Activity increased
substantially during September to November, although December 2011 is
incomplete and cannot be compared directly with complete months.

Thursday recorded the highest weekday activity across several measures.
Saturday had no observations, while Sunday activity was present.

Most recorded activity occurred between 9:00 and 16:00, with the highest
record count at 12:00.

These patterns describe the observed transactions and should not
automatically be interpreted as causal business trends or operating
hours.

    \subsection{6.5 Relationship Analysis}\label{relationship-analysis}

This section examines relationships among quantitative variables.

We focus on Quantity, UnitPrice, and LineValue.

Because the variables are highly right-skewed and contain extreme
observations, correlation coefficients must be interpreted cautiously.

LineValue is calculated as Quantity multiplied by UnitPrice. Therefore,
its relationships with those variables are partly mathematical rather
than independent business relationships.

    \subsubsection{Relationship Analysis --- Key
Findings}\label{relationship-analysis-key-findings}

Pearson and Spearman correlation coefficients reveal different aspects
of the relationships among the quantitative variables.

Quantity and UnitPrice have almost no linear correlation (Pearson:
-0.022), but show a moderate negative monotonic association (Spearman:
-0.402).

Quantity and LineValue have a strong positive association (Pearson:
0.916; Spearman: 0.684).

UnitPrice and LineValue have a weak Pearson correlation (0.061) and a
positive Spearman correlation (0.332).

These relationships must be interpreted in the context of the highly
skewed distributions and the mathematical definition of LineValue as
Quantity multiplied by UnitPrice.

Correlation does not establish causation.

    \section{7. Conclusions, Findings and
Recommendations}\label{conclusions-findings-and-recommendations}

\subsection{7.1 Executive Summary}\label{executive-summary}

This project presents an end-to-end Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA) of
the UCI Online Retail dataset.

The original dataset contains 541,909 transaction-line records and eight
variables, covering transactions from December 2010 to December 2011.

After data quality assessment, feature engineering, transaction
classification, and analytical population evaluation, a primary
analytical population of 527,948 positive-sales-candidate records was
selected.

The analysis focused on transaction characteristics, product activity,
geographic distribution, temporal patterns, quantitative distributions,
and relationships among numerical variables.

\subsubsection{Key Findings}\label{key-findings}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Geographic concentration}

  The United Kingdom accounts for approximately 91.69\% of
  positive-sales-candidate records. The five most active countries
  account for 96.82\%.
\item
  \textbf{Product activity}

  The dataset contains 3,917 distinct stock codes. Product rankings
  differ depending on whether activity is measured by record frequency,
  quantity, or line value.
\item
  \textbf{Highly skewed quantitative distributions}

  Quantity, UnitPrice, and LineValue are strongly right-skewed and
  heavy-tailed. Their means are substantially higher than their medians,
  and extreme observations have a considerable influence on summary
  statistics.
\item
  \textbf{Exceptional transactions}

  Some extreme quantities have corresponding cancellation transactions
  with equal and opposite quantities and values. These observations
  demonstrate why unusual records should be investigated rather than
  automatically removed.
\item
  \textbf{Cancellation complexity}

  Exploratory matching identified potential full invoice reversals, but
  the dataset does not provide a definitive reference linking
  cancellation invoices to original invoices. Cancellation records were
  therefore not automatically deducted from the primary analytical
  population.
\item
  \textbf{Temporal patterns}

  November 2011 recorded the highest monthly activity. Thursday had the
  highest weekday activity, while transaction records were concentrated
  between 9:00 and 16:00.
\item
  \textbf{Relationships among quantitative variables}

  Quantity and LineValue showed a strong positive Pearson correlation of
  0.916. However, LineValue is mathematically derived from Quantity and
  UnitPrice, so this relationship must be interpreted accordingly.
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Overall Conclusion}\label{overall-conclusion}

The analysis demonstrates that meaningful EDA requires more than
descriptive statistics and visualizations.

Understanding the observational unit, evaluating data quality, making
defensible analytical decisions, investigating unusual observations, and
acknowledging limitations are essential to producing reliable analytical
findings.

    \subsection{7.2 Limitations and Analytical
Considerations}\label{limitations-and-analytical-considerations}

The following limitations should be considered when interpreting the
results.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Historical dataset}

  The dataset covers December 2010 through December 2011. Its findings
  should not be assumed to represent current retail behavior.
\item
  \textbf{Incomplete customer information}

  CustomerID is missing for approximately 24.90\% of records in the
  selected analytical population. Consequently, customer-level findings
  apply only to identifiable customers.
\item
  \textbf{Transaction classification}

  Positive quantity, positive unit price, and a numeric invoice prefix
  were used to define positive-sales candidates. These conditions do not
  independently establish payment, fulfillment, or recognized revenue.
\item
  \textbf{Cancellation matching}

  Original and cancellation invoices cannot always be linked reliably.
  The analysis therefore does not claim to calculate verified net sales
  or accounting revenue.
\item
  \textbf{Extreme observations}

  Statistical outliers are not necessarily errors. Some extreme
  transactions may represent legitimate bulk purchases or subsequent
  reversals.
\item
  \textbf{Geographic concentration}

  The dataset is heavily concentrated in the United Kingdom. Findings
  from smaller geographic groups should be interpreted cautiously.
\item
  \textbf{Temporal coverage}

  December 2011 contains transactions only through December 9. Monthly
  comparisons involving this period are therefore limited.
\item
  \textbf{Observational analysis}

  The analysis identifies distributions, patterns, and associations. It
  does not establish causal relationships.
\end{enumerate}

    \subsection{7.3 Recommendations}\label{recommendations}

Based on the findings, the following recommendations are proposed for
further business investigation.

\subsubsection{1. Geographic Market
Analysis}\label{geographic-market-analysis}

The United Kingdom accounts for approximately 91.69\% of
positive-sales-candidate records.

Further analysis could investigate: - Customer purchasing behavior
across major markets. - Differences in product preferences between
countries. - Opportunities for developing smaller international markets.

\subsubsection{2. Product Performance
Monitoring}\label{product-performance-monitoring}

Product rankings vary depending on transaction frequency, quantity, and
total line value.

Product performance should therefore be evaluated using multiple
measures rather than a single ranking.

Exceptional transactions should also be investigated separately before
drawing conclusions about product demand.

\subsubsection{3. Customer Data Quality}\label{customer-data-quality}

Approximately 24.90\% of records in the selected population lack
CustomerID.

Improving customer identification could strengthen: - Customer
segmentation. - Repeat-purchase analysis. - Customer lifetime value
estimation. - Customer-level purchasing behavior analysis.

\subsubsection{4. Cancellation and Transaction
Monitoring}\label{cancellation-and-transaction-monitoring}

Cancellation records should be maintained as a separate analytical
population until reliable transaction-level matching is available.

A formal reference between original and cancellation transactions would
improve reconciliation and net-sales analysis.

\subsubsection{5. Temporal Business
Analysis}\label{temporal-business-analysis}

The observed increase in activity during September to November 2011
deserves further investigation.

Future analysis could examine: - Monthly product demand. - Seasonal
purchasing patterns. - Changes in customer activity. - Differences in
average transaction values.

These recommendations identify opportunities for further investigation.
They should not be interpreted as verified business problems or
guaranteed opportunities.

    \subsection{7.4 Final Conclusion}\label{final-conclusion}

This project demonstrates a structured, reproducible approach to
Exploratory Data Analysis using the Online Retail dataset.

The analysis progressed from initial data inspection and quality
assessment to feature engineering, analytical population selection,
quantitative and categorical exploration, temporal analysis, and
relationship analysis.

A major outcome was the selection of 527,948 positive-sales-candidate
records while preserving the distinction between candidate sales
transactions and verified completed sales.

The investigation also demonstrated the importance of examining missing
information, duplicate records, extreme observations, operational
transactions, and cancellation activity before drawing conclusions.

Rather than treating every unusual observation as an error, the analysis
used statistical evidence and transaction context to evaluate potential
explanations.

The resulting findings provide a foundation for further business
analysis while clearly documenting the limitations of the available
data.

Ultimately, this project emphasizes that effective EDA is not simply
about producing charts or calculating statistics. It is about asking
meaningful questions, making defensible analytical decisions,
interpreting evidence, and communicating results responsibly.

    \subsection{Final Validation}\label{final-validation}

This section performs final consistency checks on the analytical
population and its principal characteristics.

The checks ensure that the selected dataset retains its expected
structure and satisfies the analytical criteria established during data
preparation.

\PY{k}{assert} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{LineValue}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{notna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{all}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
